Two different line styles for two different kinds of certainty, so they're never confused for each other even though they're both drawn in the same color at the same time. The map legend's buffer-zone row was updated to show both the fill and the new solid border in its swatch.

**Verification.** Full-file syntax check, `getElementById` id-resolution check, and a div-balance check (69/69) all passed clean. 11 Node `vm` assertions against the actual extracted source: a test cutblock produces both fill squares and perimeter line segments; every line segment is confirmed solid (no `dashArray`), fully opaque, and a visible weight; a point deep inside the cutblock (definitely interior to the halo) correctly produces zero outward-facing edges in any direction (no spurious interior lines); a point near the actual 400m boundary correctly does detect an outward-facing edge; the edge-segment geometry helper produces a real 2-point line spanning exactly the given grid spacing; and the four cardinal-direction offsets (N/S/E/W) all point the correct way. All 11 passed.

## v53: a "Clear Potential Spots" button
Karim's request: *"Let's create a button that can clear the shooting area parcels."* A third button, "✖ Clear Potential Spots," now sits under "Show Potential Spots (this view)" in the map-actions panel (top-right). It empties `spotLayer` — every teal/purple spot, active-cutblock outline, and buffer zone the feature has drawn — and resets the `spotDetails`/`gapDetails` arrays the popups' "i" buttons index into, so nothing is left pointing at stale detail text if the feature is run again afterward. The status note updates to confirm the clear happened and to point back at the "Show" button.

Scoped deliberately to just the Potential Spots layer — "Reveal Crown Land" (a separate button, separate layer) is untouched, since Karim's wording was specifically about the shooting-spot shapes, not the plain Crown-land tint. If a "clear everything" button (both layers at once) would actually be more useful in practice, that's a one-line addition on top of this.

**Why this is worth having**: previously the only way to remove Potential Spots' shapes from the map was to press "Show Potential Spots" again over a different view (which replaces them) — there was no way to just declutter the current view without either re-querying or reloading the page.

**Also answered in chat, not a code change**: what it means when a parcel *isn't* highlighted by Potential Spots. Only two things ever get drawn by that feature — confirmed Crown/untitled parcels that clear every hard exclusion and (at close enough zoom) the building/road buffer, and "gap" points with no ParcelMap record at all. Everything else in view — private, municipal, federal, First Nations parcels; Crown/untitled parcels that overlap a park or municipality; Crown/untitled parcels the buffer check trimmed away entirely — is deliberately left undrawn, not a bug or a missed query. "Reveal Crown Land" draws a broader set (any Crown/untitled parcel, none of Potential Spots' extra exclusion/buffer logic) — comparing what's highlighted between the two buttons on the same view is the fastest way to see exactly which filter took a given parcel out.

**Verification.** Full-file syntax check, `getElementById` id-resolution check (58 declared ids now, up from 57 — the new button — all 45 unique `getElementById` calls still resolve), and a div-balance check (69/69) all passed clean. 6 Node `vm` assertions against the actual extracted `clearPotentialSpots()` function confirmed: `spotLayer.clearLayers()` is actually invoked; `spotDetails`/`gapDetails` both reset to empty arrays from a populated starting state; the status note text updates to mention the clear. All passed.

## v52: two-layer rebalance — solid real outline, lighter approximate fill
Karim's request, verbatim: *"Let's change the cut block diameter so that the outline is 100% solid and the inside is back set to 0.20."* Read as: the real cutblock's own boundary (the dashed outline, drawn from actual FTEN geometry) should read as fully solid/opaque, and the approximate 400m buffer-zone fill (the grid-sampled halo, drawn once per cutblock since v48) should go back down to a lighter 0.20.

Two one-line changes: `activeCutblockOutlineStyle()`'s `opacity` went from 0.9 to 1 (the outline was already visually distinct via its dashed line style — this makes it fully opaque too, no fade at all). `cutblockBufferStyle()`'s `fillOpacity` went from 0.5 (v51) down to 0.20 — lighter than either v50 or v51, closer to where it started at v48's original 0.16. The map legend swatch was updated to match (0.2 opacity).

This is a sensible cartographic pairing, worth naming explicitly: the *real, surveyed* shape (the cutblock's actual polygon) now gets the strongest, crispest treatment — solid, fully opaque, dashed line — while the *approximate, inferred* shape (the 400m buffer, built from grid-sampling since it isn't a true polygon offset) stays visually softer. That's the right way around — precision should look more certain than approximation, not the reverse — and it should also make the two elements easier to tell apart at a glance than the last two rounds' single-opacity tuning did.

**Verification.** The two edits are isolated numeric-literal changes inside existing, already-tested style functions (no new logic, no new call sites) — confirmed correct by direct inspection of both object literals (valid syntax, correct property names, matching braces). This round's automated syntax/id-resolution/div-balance pass could not be run due to a transient tool-execution outage in this session; given the change's small, mechanical scope, that risk is low, but the full automated pass should be run at the start of the next round before any further edits stack on top of these two, per this project's normal practice.

## v51: cutblock buffer opacity bumped again, 0.35 → 0.5
Karim's direct follow-up to v50: *"let's bump up that opacity to 0.5."* One-line change — `cutblockBufferStyle()`'s `fillOpacity` moved from 0.35 to 0.5, the map legend swatch updated to match. Everything else (the 400m distance, the non-interactive squares, no border) untouched. Full verification (syntax, id-resolution, div-balance — 69/69) passed clean; no unit tests needed beyond that, since this is a single style-literal change with no logic to regress.

## v50: shorter cutblock buffer distance, more solid red
Karim's request, verbatim: *"Let's reduce the radius to 400m for cut blocks and make the red less translucent."*

A tuning pass on v48's buffer-zone feature, no behavioral redesign: `CUTBLOCK_WARN_DISTANCE_M` dropped from 500 to 400 (the single constant everything else — `renderActiveCutblockBuffers()`'s grid sampling, the oversized-cutblock exclusion's bbox expansion, the status-note text, the cutblock popup text — already reads from, so this was a one-line change with everything downstream following automatically), and the shaded zone's fill opacity roughly doubled, from 0.16 to 0.35 (border stays at `weight: 0` — still no outline seam between adjacent buffer squares, just a more solid fill). The map legend swatch and the active-cutblock popup's own explanatory text were updated to match (the popup text now interpolates the constant directly instead of a hardcoded "500m", so a future distance change won't leave stale text behind the way this round found a few in the code comments).

**Housekeeping.** A handful of code comments describing the *current* mechanism in present tense (not v45-v48 historical narrative, which correctly still says "500m" because that was true at the time) had the number hardcoded and were now wrong — fixed to describe the buffer distance generically ("the buffer zone," "its configured buffer distance") rather than repeating a number that can change, so a future tuning pass like this one doesn't leave the comments stale again.

**Verification.** Full-file syntax check, `getElementById` id-resolution check, and a div-balance check (69/69) all passed clean. 7 Node `vm` assertions against the actual extracted source: the constant reads 400; `cutblockBufferStyle()` returns the new 0.35 opacity with weight still 0; a test cutblock's buffer halo now measurably stays within 400m (previously would have extended toward 500m) while still meaningfully extending outward, not collapsing to nothing; and the active-cutblock popup's own text reads "400m buffer zone" — dynamically, from the constant — with no leftover "500m" text. All passed.

**Not a design change** — this is Karim's own visual/distance tuning on top of v48's already-approved buffer-zone approach, not a new root-cause fix or judgment call, so nothing here second-guesses the underlying mechanism.

## v49: an unusually large active cutblock is excluded from Potential Spots entirely
Karim's direct follow-up to v48: *"any cut block that is triggered as unusually large should not be considered in the shooting spot feature."*

**What this reuses, not reinvents.** The app has had an "is this cutblock record implausibly huge for a single block" sanity check since v26 — `CUTBLOCK_AREA_SANITY_HA` (7500 ha), grounded in real numbers Karim supplied at the time (BC's own FREP data puts the province-wide average cutblock at ~23 ha, with a 40/60 ha regulatory cap; the threshold is set 125x above the larger of those, generous enough to never flag a real block while still catching the actual 187,690 ha FTEN record Karim reported back then — almost certainly a licence/operating-area boundary mislabeled as a single "cut block" in BC's own data, not a bug in this app's query). That check already excludes an oversized entry from the single-click report's map fill (while still listing it in the report text, per this project's "never silently omit" rule). It had never been applied to the *Potential Spots* active-cutblock feature, though — `activeCuts` was built straight from the raw FTEN query, size unchecked.

**The fix.** Pulled the inline oversized test out of `buildCutblockList()` into its own shared function, `isOversizedCutblock(geometry, claimedAreaHa)` — same logic as before (checks the polygon's own computed area first via `geomAreaHectares()`, falls back to the claimed `PLANNED_GROSS_BLOCK_AREA`/`OPENING_GROSS_AREA` attribute), just no longer duplicated as two copies of the same magic-number comparison. `buildCutblockList()` now calls it instead of repeating the check inline (no behavior change there — same threshold, same test, just one source of truth). `revealPotentialSpots()`'s `activeCuts` now filters through the same function before anything downstream (the buffer-zone renderer, the outline renderer) ever sees a candidate cutblock — an oversized active record gets no outline, no buffer zone, and no say in the reveal at all, exactly matching Karim's "should not be considered" wording rather than just being drawn more cautiously.

**Not silent about it.** Per this app's established philosophy of never quietly dropping data, the Potential Spots status note now says how many active cutblocks were excluded this way and why, e.g. *"1 active cutblock record flagged as unusually large (>7,500 ha — almost certainly a mislabeled licence/operating-area boundary, not a real single cutblock) and left out of the buffer-zone check entirely."* — so if Karim ever wonders why an active block he can see in BC's own data isn't showing a buffer zone here, the note explains it rather than leaving it a mystery.

**Verification.** Full-file syntax check, `getElementById` id-resolution check, and a div-balance check (69/69) all passed clean. Two Node `vm` test batches against the actual extracted source: 9 assertions directly on `isOversizedCutblock()` (a realistic ~50ha block via geometry alone is not flagged; a 10,000ha geometry is flagged even with no claimed-area attribute; a normal-sized geometry with a huge claimed area is flagged via the attribute fallback; no geometry and no claimed area fails safe rather than crashing; the boundary is strictly-greater-than, so exactly 7,500ha is not flagged and 7,501 is; a non-numeric claimed area is ignored safely) — all passed. 6 further assertions on the actual `activeCuts`-building code copied verbatim from the live file: a mixed set of active/retired/pending blocks, some oversized, correctly keeps only the normal-sized *active* one and counts exactly the oversized *active* one (not the equally-huge but non-active block, which was never going to be considered anyway); an all-oversized input correctly empties `activeCuts` while the count still reflects it; a failed or missing `cutPlanRes` still returns empty/zero without crashing — all passed.

**Still open, needs Karim's own eyes:** whether 7,500 ha remains the right cutoff now that it's gating *exclusion from the feature* rather than just *exclusion from a map fill* — the original threshold was chosen generously so it would never catch a real block in the report-list use case; if a genuinely enormous (but real) cutblock ever gets excluded from Potential Spots' buffer feature by this same generous threshold, that's a reasonable trade worth knowing about, not a design Karim was asked to sign off on for this specific use.

## v48: one 500m buffer zone per active cutblock, replacing the per-spot check entirely
Karim's follow-up on v47, with the actual root cause identified: *"I see the problem now, it's because of the extremely large cut block. It's interfering causing the parcels to be labeled red while we were ignoring this large of a cut block before. Also instead of just labeling parcels based on the cut block radius lets just make a 500m perimeter around each cut block that's active instead."*

**Confirms the v47 theory, with the specifics filled in.** v47's working theory — a large/elongated active cutblock's 500m halo blanketing a wide area — is exactly what Karim traced on his end. His framing sharpens *why* it happened: the check was being run from the wrong side. Every nearby spot independently sampled its own grid and asked "am I within 500m of any active cutblock," which means one unusually large cutblock got measured against differently by every spot around it — the flagged area was a byproduct of how the *spots* happened to be gridded, not a direct picture of the cutblock's own real footprint plus 500m.

**The fix: invert which side computes the buffer.** Rather than every spot asking "am I near a cutblock," each active cutblock now asks, once, "what does my own 500m halo look like" — grid-sampled directly around its own polygon and drawn as a single shaded zone (`renderActiveCutblockBuffers()`, replacing the removed `computeCutblockWarnInfo()`). This is a real behavior change, not just a rendering tweak: the per-spot proximity check, the popup's "⚠ active cutblock Xm" pill, and its expanded detail text are all gone now — a spot's popup no longer says anything about cutblock distance at all. Whether a spot is affected is now purely visual: does its shape fall inside the shaded zone, or under the cutblock's own dashed outline (kept from v46, drawn on top of the new shaded zone so the real block boundary stays visible through it). This is a deliberate simplification, not an oversight — Karim's wording ("instead of... just make... instead") asked for a replacement, and duplicating the same info as both a visual and a redundant text pill would have been clutter now that the zone is directly visible.

**Why this actually fixes the "too much red" report, not just moves it.** The computation is now anchored to the cutblock's own bbox (expanded by 500m) instead of to however many nearby spots happen to be in view — so a huge cutblock produces exactly one halo, sized to its own real shape, computed once, regardless of how many (or how few) candidate spots surround it. Cost also dropped from "every spot's own grid × every nearby cutblock" to "every active cutblock's own grid, once" — cheaper, not just cleaner. The buffer is approximated with the same grid-sampling technique this app uses everywhere else for buffers/clips (a true polygon offset/buffer is the same kind of hard-to-verify-blind geometry operation this project has consistently avoided — see `clipParcelByInfrastructure()`), using the coarser viewport-scale grid density (40–400m adaptive spacing, the same machinery v44's gap detection already uses) rather than the finer per-parcel grid, specifically so a large cutblock's buffer stays a bounded number of squares instead of scaling with its own huge area.

**Verification.** Full-file syntax check, `getElementById` id-resolution check, and a div-balance check (69/69) all passed clean. Grepped the whole file for `computeCutblockWarnInfo`, `cutInfo`, and `nearbyActiveCuts` (the removed per-spot machinery) — zero remaining references outside one intentional explanatory comment. Two new Node `vm` test batches against the actual extracted source (not stubs): 14 assertions on `renderActiveCutblockBuffers()` — a small cutblock produces a real halo; no active cuts or missing geometry draw nothing and don't crash; every drawn square is verified to actually be within 500m of the cutblock's real polygon (not just its bbox); the halo genuinely extends out toward the 500m edge rather than clustering near the block; squares are styled with no border and non-interactive (so they never steal a click from the outline or a spot underneath); and specifically for the large-cutblock scenario, a 3km-elongated test block still produces a bounded square count using the coarse 40–400m spacing rather than exploding — all 14 passed. 9 further assertions confirmed `spotPopup()`/`gapPopup()`/their detail HTML no longer reference cutblocks at all, still correctly report advisory counts, and still escape injected HTML in advisory/owner-type text (the same XSS regression check from v46) — all 9 passed.

**Still open, needs Karim's own eyes:** the shaded zone's visual weight (0.16 fill opacity, no border) against the dashed outline and the teal/purple spot colors, at the zoom levels he actually works at — the specific number was chosen to be noticeably present without repeating v45's "buries the map" problem, but that's a judgment call worth his own look, not something verifiable from here.

## v47: dropped the filled red 500m cutblock-proximity squares
Karim's report, with two screenshots of a live view near Cloudburst Mountain: *"This is what I'm seeing, it seems like there are too many red blocks as there are spots in the middle there that are not close to main roads, cut blocks, or buildings."* The screenshots showed a dense field of overlapping squares (mostly the v44 purple gap-detection grid) with a wide swath tinted red, plus one long dashed maroon line running across the whole visible viewport — almost certainly v46's active-cutblock outline, which strongly suggested a single large or elongated active cutblock was involved.

**Is it a bug?** Checked first, since "too much red" is exactly what a distance-math bug would look like. It isn't one: v45 already had unit tests confirming `pointToGeomMeters()` is exact (a point inside a polygon returns 0; a point offset by a known distance returns that distance, not an approximation), and the actual per-point check the red squares were gated on (`d < CUTBLOCK_WARN_DISTANCE_M`) uses that same exact function — the bbox pre-filter (`nearbyActiveCuts()`) is only a coarse candidate-reduction step before the real check, not the check itself. Re-reading the code turned up nothing wrong with the math.

**What was actually wrong: the design, not the logic.** 500m is a wide radius relative to a real BC cutblock's own footprint. Painting a filled square at *every* grid sample point within 500m of a large or elongated active cutblock's boundary is mathematically correct at each individual point, but the union of all those squares can legitimately blanket a wide swath of the viewport — which is exactly "too many red blocks... in the middle... not close to main roads, cut blocks, or buildings" from Karim's own vantage point, even though every one of those squares really was within 500m of *something* active. It reads as broken because a 500m halo drawn as filled tiles doesn't look like "close to one specific cutblock" — it looks like noise.

**The fix.** Per the working-style guidelines Karim set in v45 — take ownership of judgment calls, push back on a weak design rather than just re-tweaking numbers or asking another clarifying question — the filled-square overlay was removed entirely, both from the confirmed-parcel path and the gap-point path. The warning is now communicated only two ways, both already present from v46 and both untouched by this change: the dashed active-cutblock outline drawn on the map (so the *actual* cutblock shape, not an inflated 500m halo around it, is what's visible), and the spot's own popup pill/detail text (still names the exact triggering block ID(s) and nearest distance). Nothing about *whether* a spot gets flagged changed — same 500m threshold, same exact-distance math, same block-ID/distance detail text — only the square-painting side effect that made a normal, correct result look like noise on screen.

**Verification.** Full-file syntax check, `getElementById` id-resolution check, and a div-balance check all passed clean (68 opening/68 closing `<div>` tags, depth never negative). Re-extracted and re-ran the renamed function (`renderCutblockWarnOverlay()` → `computeCutblockWarnInfo()`, `targetLayer` parameter dropped) against 11 Node `vm` assertions: no active cuts nearby returns null; a cut far outside 500m returns null via the bbox pre-filter; a cut immediately adjacent returns the correct ID and a near-zero distance; a cut just past the 500m threshold correctly returns null; `nearbyActiveCuts()` still accepts a bare point as well as a bbox; the new outline style has `fillOpacity: 0` (confirms no filled shape survived); and, as a direct check against regression, the function's own source contains no `L.geoJSON` call and no `targetLayer` reference — the drawing code is actually gone, not just unused. All 11 passed. Grepped the whole file for any remaining reference to the removed `cutblockWarnStyle()` function or the old `renderCutblockWarnOverlay()` name — one stale code comment was found and fixed; none remain.

**Still open, needs Karim's own eyes:** whether the dashed outline (now the only visual signal) clicked open on that specific viewport shows a large/elongated ACTIVE block near where the red was densest — which would confirm the "big cutblock, wide halo" theory outright — or something else. Also open: whether this lighter-touch treatment (outline + popup text only) reads better in practice, or whether some other visual (e.g. a translucent glow only right at the cutblock's own edge, rather than a 500m halo) would communicate "you're close to an active cutblock" more clearly without repeating the same problem.

## v46: condensed popups behind an "i" button, plus visible active-cutblock outlines
Karim's request, two parts: *"Take out in depth descriptions in the pop ups only provide high yield information anything else should go in an 'i' icon. Also it seems the red highlighted parcels are inaccurate, there isn't an active cut block within 500m of it only retired ones."*

**Condensed popups.** The `spotPopup()`/`gapPopup()` map popups added in v44/v45 had grown into multi-paragraph explanations every time (buffer distances, the Wildlife-Act-vs-Karim's-margin caveat, advisories, the cutblock-warning explainer). These are now two or three lines — owner type/PID, an advisory-count pill, and (new) a cutblock-distance pill when relevant — with a small "i" button that opens the rest in the same shared `openInfoModal()` popup this app already uses everywhere else (the header info button, sources, checklist help, and the v34 cutblock-source-info button this exact pattern is modeled on). Mechanically: each popup's full detail HTML is pushed onto a small per-reveal array (`spotDetails`/`gapDetails`, reset at the start of every `revealPotentialSpots()` run) and the "i" button's `onclick` references it by index — the same index-into-array technique `showMapCutblockDetail(i)` already used for cutblock popups, chosen specifically to avoid trying to cram a large HTML string into an inline `onclick` attribute.

**The "inaccurate red parcels" report.** Karim saw a red 500m warning square next to what he could tell was a *retired* cutblock, not an active one. Two honest things about this: first, this session has no live network access to BC's actual WFS server to pull real data and directly reproduce what Karim saw — everything here is reasoned from the code and BC's documented field semantics, not confirmed against the live query that actually ran in his browser. Second, the most likely explanation isn't a bug in the ACTIVE filter itself (re-checked carefully — it only ever operates on `activeCuts`, already filtered to `LIFE_CYCLE_STATUS_CODE === "ACTIVE"` before anything downstream touches it) but a **visibility gap**: before this round, Potential Spots never drew the FTEN cutblocks it was checking distance against — the 500m check ran entirely invisibly. So what Karim most likely saw was a genuinely retired cutblock he happened to notice nearby, while a *different*, genuinely active FTEN record he had no way to see was the actual trigger within 500m. It's also possible for a single physical block to carry a RESULTS-side "retired-looking" status while its FTEN cutting-permit record is still administratively ACTIVE ("approved; activities may be taking place" is a permit-status field, not a live logging-activity feed) — these are two different BC datasets tracking two different things, and they don't always agree.

Rather than guess at a fix for a report that can't be reproduced from here, this round makes the warning **self-verifying**: every active FTEN cutblock in the current view is now drawn on the map as its own dashed red outline (no fill, so it's never mistaken for a spot), clickable for a small popup confirming its block ID and ACTIVE status. Every red warning square's popup detail now also names the exact block ID(s) and nearest distance that triggered it. If Karim sees a warning next to a block whose own outline/popup shows RETIRED, that's now a reproducible, reportable specific (which exact block ID, at which location) instead of a general "this seems off" — the fastest way to actually find and fix a real bug if one exists, versus a data-source disagreement, which would call for different handling (asking which field Karim considers authoritative).

**Verification.** Full-file syntax check, `getElementById` id-resolution check, and a div-balance check (209/209) all passed clean. Five Node `vm` unit tests against the actual extracted popup-building functions (not stubs): a popup with no cutblock warning shows no cutblock text at all; one with a warning shows the correctly-rounded nearest-distance pill and advisory count; the "i" button's array index matches a real, present detail entry; `gapPopup()`'s equivalent works the same way; and — since advisory/ID text can in principle contain characters that would otherwise break out of the popup HTML — a `<script>` payload in an advisory string comes back HTML-escaped in the detail output, not executable. All five passed.

**Not yet visually confirmed** — same standing caveat as the rest of this feature: whether the condensed popups read well at a glance, and whether the newly-visible active-cutblock outlines actually explain what Karim saw (or reveal a real bug once he can see the specific block ID involved), both need his own eyes on a live view.

## v45: wider buffers (incl. private land), plus a non-excluding cutblock-proximity warning
This round answered v44's open question — Karim's specific rule change for Potential Spots — plus a general working-style request that isn't specific to this app, handled first below.

**General preference, not app-specific.** Karim asked that Claude, across every product/session (not just this chat), work as an engaged expert collaborator rather than a literal instruction-taker — pushing back on weak assumptions, diagnosing root causes instead of surface symptoms, applying the judgment of someone who's handled the situation before, improving his existing work critically instead of leaving it alone by default, and taking ownership of judgment calls rather than just executing. Saved to Claude's cross-session preferences so it isn't scoped to this project.

**The rule change, verbatim:** Karim's own numbers, adjusted from what this app's own report described back to him:
- Confirmed Crown/untitled parcels and no-data gap points (v44) both still work the same way structurally — same ownership test, same "not confirmed vs. inferred" distinction.
- Hard exclusions (park, municipality) — unchanged, no exceptions.
- The Wildlife Act buffer check — Karim widened two of the three distances past what's actually written in the cited regulation, as his own personal safety margin: **600m from buildings *and now also private-titled land*** (was 100m, buildings only), 25m from ordinary roads (unchanged), **600m from the named-highway list** (was 400m). A spot that clears none of it is still dropped entirely; a partly-clear one is still trimmed to just the clear portion, same mechanism as before.
- Tenure/woodlot/cutblock overlaps still don't exclude a spot — but as of v45, an *active* cutblock specifically (not any cutblock) within 500m no longer stays a text-only advisory: the overlapping portion of the spot now gets a visible red warning overlay on the map itself.
- Below the tighter zoom, the buffer check is still skipped with the same explanatory note (now mentioning private land too).

**Buffers now also cover private land.** "600m from buildings and private land" meant widening what counts as an exclusion-buffer source, not just the distance. Previously the building buffer only ever checked against OpenStreetMap building footprints; it now also checks against every private-titled parcel in the viewport (`ownerCategory()==='private'`), sourced from the same all-owner-types parcel query v44 already added for gap detection — no new network query needed, just a new filter on data already being fetched. Both sources are combined into one ring list and tested at the same distance, in both the confirmed-parcel path and the gap-point path.

**A word on "per the Wildlife Act."** The 100m/25m/400m figures were originally cited directly from the BC Hunting and Trapping Regulations Synopsis Karim supplied — real legal minimums, not invented. Karim's 600m/25m/600m ask goes past two of those. Rather than just relabeling 600m as "the law" (which it isn't), the code comments, click-report text, and map popups all now say plainly that these are Karim's own chosen margin, at or above the cited legal minimums, not the minimums themselves — so nobody downstream mistakes 600m for a legal citation. The 25m road figure is untouched and still matches the cited minimum exactly.

**The cutblock proximity warning, and how "overlap in red" got implemented.** Karim's wording was somewhat open to two readings: recolor the whole spot red, or mark only the overlapping sub-area. Given the explicit "will not exclude the parcel or potential shooting spot" and "overlap... where the potential shooting spot is within 500m," this was built as an **overlay**, not a recolor: the spot keeps its normal teal/purple shape, color, and popup exactly as before, and a separate, non-interactive translucent red grid layer is drawn on top wherever a sample point of that spot falls within 500m of an active cutblock. (`interactive:false` on the overlay squares so they never intercept a click meant for the spot underneath.) If this isn't the visual Karim pictured, it's a straightforward swap to full-shape recoloring instead — flagged as a judgment call, not a locked-in design.

"Active" specifically means BC's own `LIFE_CYCLE_STATUS_CODE = ACTIVE` ("approved; activities may be taking place" — see the v29 section below for where this field was first confirmed). That field only lives on the FTEN cutting-permit layer, not the RESULTS layer `revealPotentialSpots()` already queries for the tenure/woodlot/cutblock advisory text — so this reveal now also queries FTEN (`LAYERS.cutblockPlan`) specifically to get it, independent of and in addition to the existing RESULTS query.

**Verification.** Full-file syntax check, `getElementById` id-resolution check, and a div-balance check (196/196) all passed clean. Two new Node `vm` unit-test batches, run against the actual extracted source: `pointToGeomMeters()` — the new polygon-distance helper (3 assertions: a point inside a polygon returns exactly 0, a point a known ~1113m outside a square returns a distance in the expected range, confirming the equirectangular meters math carries over correctly from the existing point-to-ring helper it's built on) — all passed; `nearbyActiveCuts()`'s bbox pre-filter (2 assertions: correctly finds a cutblock near a given point and correctly excludes one that's nowhere close, and separately confirmed it accepts either a point or a 4-value bbox as input) — all passed.

**Not yet visually confirmed** — same standing caveat as v40/v43/v44: the red overlay's actual look against a real map (especially whether it reads clearly at typical zoom levels, and whether "overlay, not recolor" turns out to be what Karim actually wanted to see) hasn't been exercised in a browser yet.

## v44: "no parcel record" now counts as a spot, plus a Mapbox usage safety cap
Karim's request, three parts: *"Let's narrow down the rules for what makes a potential shooting spot. Also when looking for a shooting spot let's include parcels that have no data on them. Another thing to do: Implement mapbox. Make sure to pull the amount of calls made so that we don't exceed 50000, if we do then switch it back to the orignial map automatically."*

Two clarifying questions went out before building anything. On "narrow the rules," Karim picked "Something else" without elaborating what — that part is **parked, not implemented**, since guessing at a rule change felt riskier than asking again (see "Not yet done" below). On "no data" parcels, Karim chose **both** offered interpretations, so both were built:

**No-parcel-record points now count as a spot, not an inconclusive result.** Previously, when ParcelMap returns zero features at the exact clicked point, `renderReport()` filed that under `reasons.gray` — the same bucket as "the query itself failed" — which pushed the overall verdict to "❔ Inconclusive" unless something else on the same click was red/amber/green. That's now `reasons.green`, worded the same way the existing Crown-provincial/untitled-provincial cases are, but explicitly flagged as an *inference from absence* rather than a positive government record (unlike those two, which come from ParcelMap's own OWNER_TYPE field) — because a true parcel-fabric gap and a click that landed in open water or offshore look identical from this data alone, and Karim should be able to tell that apart from a confirmed Crown/untitled parcel at a glance.

**Potential Spots now also surfaces those gaps on the map.** This is the harder half. DataBC has no "give me everywhere NOT covered by a parcel" query, and a real polygon subtraction (viewport minus every parcel's shape) is exactly the kind of complex, hard-to-verify-blind geometry this project has deliberately steered around before (see the buffer-clipping section above, and the BBOX saga further down). So this reuses that same established grid-sampling philosophy instead of solving polygon subtraction: `revealPotentialSpots()` now also fetches *every* parcel in the current view regardless of owner type (a second, unfiltered ParcelMap query, capped at 3000 features), lays an adaptive point grid across the whole viewport (40–400m spacing, budgeted to ~500 points total regardless of how wide the view is — coarser than the existing 15–150m per-parcel grid, which only ever covers one parcel's much smaller area), and keeps the sample points that fall inside *no* parcel at all. Each surviving point goes through the exact same park/municipality exclusion and (when zoomed in enough) Wildlife Act road/building buffer check that a regular Crown/untitled parcel spot already gets, then draws as a small purple square — a different color (`#8a5cf5`) and dash pattern from the teal confirmed-Crown/untitled parcels, both in the map itself and in a new map-legend row, so the two kinds of evidence stay visually distinct. The status note under the buttons now reports gap-spot counts as their own sentence, including an explicit caveat when the unfiltered parcel query hit its cap (meaning some real parcels may not have been in the comparison set, so a few "gap" squares could technically have an actual record).

**Mapbox: local usage tracking with an automatic safety-cap fallback.** Karim asked to track how many calls are being made against Mapbox's usage and switch back to the original map automatically if it crosses 50,000. Two things worth flagging: first, there's no API a page holding only a Mapbox *public* token can call to pull the account's real, authoritative usage count — that number only lives on Mapbox's own dashboard — so this instead has Leaflet count its own tile requests (via each tile layer's `tileloadstart` event, which fires once per tile fetch attempted) and persists that count in `localStorage`, reset automatically at the start of each calendar month. It's a local estimate of what this browser has asked for, not a live pull from Mapbox's billing system, but it's the practical substitute and stays conservative since it counts every attempt, not just confirmed successes. Second: this session double-checked the actual free-tier number (since v42's original note here — "50,000 map loads/month" — turned out to be the wrong metric). This app requests tiles via a plain `L.tileLayer` URL, not the Mapbox GL JS SDK, which Mapbox bills under the **Static Tiles API**: **200,000 free tile requests/month** as of September 2026 (confirmed live against [mapbox.com/pricing](https://www.mapbox.com/pricing) and [docs.mapbox.com/accounts/guides/pricing](https://docs.mapbox.com/accounts/guides/pricing/) — "map loads" is a different unit that only applies to the GL JS SDK, which this app doesn't use). Karim's 50,000 figure is kept exactly as he specified it — `MAPBOX_MONTHLY_CAP` — as a deliberate safety margin well under the real 200,000 limit, not because 50,000 is the actual free-tier ceiling; the in-file comment explains this so it isn't mistaken for the real number later, and raising `MAPBOX_MONTHLY_CAP` is a one-line change if Karim ever wants more headroom.

Mechanically: `MAPBOX_ENABLED` is now a reassignable `let`, computed at page load from `MAPBOX_TOKEN_VALID && readMapboxUsage().count < MAPBOX_MONTHLY_CAP` — so if a prior session already hit the cap this month, the page opens straight onto the Esri/OpenStreetMap basemaps without making even one more Mapbox request first. `ESRI_FALLBACK_LAYERS` (the original three layers) and `MAPBOX_LAYERS` (Mapbox's three, when the token looks valid) are both always constructed; `BASE_LAYERS` starts as a plain-object copy of whichever set is active. The moment `bumpMapboxUsage()` sees the count cross the cap, `disableMapboxAndFallback()` swaps whichever basemap is currently showing to its Esri/OpenStreetMap equivalent immediately (not just on the next toggle click), points all three `BASE_LAYERS` entries at the fallback set for the rest of the month, re-tints the Crown/untitled parcel layer for the new basemap's contrast, and posts a plain-language note in the map-actions area explaining what happened and that it'll switch back automatically next month.

**Verification.** Full-file syntax check (`new Function()` over every `<script>` block), `getElementById` id-resolution check, and a div balance check (193/193) all passed clean. Two Node `vm` unit-test batches, both run against the actual extracted source (not a re-typed copy): the usage-tracking logic (6 assertions — a fresh month starts at 0, an existing same-month count carries forward, a stale month resets to 0, bumping below the cap doesn't trigger the fallback, bumping to exactly 50,000 triggers it exactly once, bumping past an already-over count still triggers it) all passed; the gap-grid geometry helpers (5 assertions — spacing clamps to the 40m floor on a tiny viewport and the 400m ceiling on a huge one, a mid-size viewport lands strictly between them, every sampled point falls inside the given bounds with the total count reasonably close to the ~500 budget, and point-in-any-geometry correctly finds a point inside one of two candidate polygons and correctly misses a point inside neither) all passed.

**Not yet done — narrowing the Potential Spots rules.** Karim's first ask this round was still open at the time of writing: he picked "Something else" on the clarifying question about what should exclude a parcel that currently doesn't, without saying what. Rather than guess (the offered options were increasing the road/building buffers, hard-excluding parcels with an overlapping tenure/recent cutblock instead of just flagging them as advisories, and filtering out small/sliver parcels), this is parked until he says which one (or something else entirely) he actually wants.

**Not yet visually confirmed** — same standing caveat as v40's draw tools and v43's resize handle: the gap-detection grid, the purple squares' appearance against a real map, and the Mapbox auto-fallback's live behavior (including whether `tileloadstart` fires the way this assumes across different browsers/network conditions) haven't been exercised in an actual browser, only reasoned about and unit-tested at the logic level.

## v43: resizable bottom drawer + matching translucency on both drawers
Karim's request: *"Let's make the bottom collapsable box resizable and make both side and bottom boxes slightly transparent."* Two changes:

**Resizable bottom drawer.** A small grip bar (`#bpResizeHandle`) now spans the top edge of the bottom report drawer, above its collapse/expand handle, always visible whether the drawer is open or collapsed. Dragging it up or down (mouse or touch, via Pointer Events — `pointerdown`/`pointermove`/`pointerup`/`pointercancel` with `setPointerCapture`) resizes the drawer live, tracking the cursor 1:1: the new height is computed as the distance from the pointer to the bottom of the viewport, then clamped by a small `clampBottomPanelHeight()` helper to a minimum of 160px (so it can't be dragged down to nothing) and a maximum of 85% of the viewport height (so it can't swallow the whole screen). Grabbing the handle while the drawer is collapsed opens it in the same motion, bottom-sheet style. The chosen height is written to `localStorage` (key `bcShootingChecker_bottomPanelHeight_v1`) only on drag-end, not on every pointer move, and restored on page load — so a preferred size sticks around. A `.resizing` class is toggled on the drawer during the drag for a future hook (currently just prevents any transition from fighting the live drag). `.bp-body`, the scrollable content area inside the drawer, was already missing `min-height:0`, which would have kept it flex-forcing the drawer back to its content's natural height instead of actually honoring a smaller dragged height — the same "flex child won't shrink below its content" class of bug this app's notes have hit before (v22-era `.layout`/`#panel` CSS); fixed alongside the resize work since the resize feature would otherwise not have visibly worked at all when dragged shorter.

**Matching translucency.** The bottom drawer was previously nearly opaque (`rgba(255,255,255,.97)`, 8px blur) while the side checklist drawer was already translucent from v41 (`rgba(255,255,255,.86)`, 12px blur) — two different "floating glass" looks. Both are now standardized to the same `rgba(255,255,255,.88)` background with a 10px blur, plus matching soft borders/shadows, so the two drawers read as one consistent treatment over the map rather than two different ones.

**Verification.** Full-file syntax check (`new Function()` extraction over every `<script>` block) passed clean. `getElementById` id-resolution check confirmed every id referenced in JS — including the new `bpResizeHandle` — resolves to exactly one element in the HTML. A div open/close balance check (187/187, running-depth walk never negative) confirmed the new `.bp-resize-handle` element didn't break the HTML structure. `clampBottomPanelHeight()` was extracted and unit-tested standalone in a Node `vm` sandbox against stubbed `window.innerHeight` values at two different viewport sizes: confirmed the floor clamps low drags to 160px, the ceiling clamps high drags to exactly 85% of viewport height (including the boundary itself), and ordinary in-range values pass through unchanged.

**Not yet visually confirmed** — like the v40 draw tools, drag-to-resize is inherently a live-pointer-events interaction that can't be meaningfully exercised in a Node `vm` sandbox; worth a look in an actual browser to confirm the drag feels smooth, the handle's cursor/hover affordance reads clearly, and the two drawers' new translucency level looks right against a real map (both light and dark basemap backgrounds, and — if Karim's Mapbox token from v42 is in place — against Mapbox tiles specifically).

## v42: optional Mapbox basemaps, gated behind Karim's own token
Karim's request, made mid-turn during the v41 layout work: *"also let's use mapbox maps instead of the current ones."* Clarified two things before building: (1) whether he had a Mapbox account/token yet -- he asked whether the API is free rather than answering directly, so that was answered first (Mapbox's free tier covers up to 50,000 map loads/month, comfortably enough for personal use -- [mapbox.com/pricing](https://www.mapbox.com/pricing)) -- and (2) scope, where Karim chose "just swap the basemap tiles" over a full rebuild on Mapbox GL JS (replacing Leaflet itself as the mapping engine, which would have meant rebuilding every overlay, popup, and the v40 draw-tools feature for a different API -- far riskier for no benefit here). Creating a Mapbox account/token is an account-signup step this tool doesn't do on Karim's behalf, so it's on him -- free at [account.mapbox.com](https://account.mapbox.com).

**Where the token goes.** Karim asked whether pasting the token into the chat itself was a safety concern -- a fair question. The answer given: Mapbox's *public* tokens (the ones starting `pk.`) are specifically designed to be embedded in client-side code like this file's own source -- that's what "public" means for this kind of token, unlike a *secret* token (`sk.`) which genuinely shouldn't be exposed. Pasting a public token into the chat is a separate question from that, though (it ends up in this conversation's history, not just the file), so Karim opted to self-paste it directly into the file instead of sending it through the conversation -- a placeholder line was added for exactly that:
```
const MAPBOX_TOKEN = "PASTE_YOUR_MAPBOX_PUBLIC_TOKEN_HERE";
```
This sits right after `const map = L.map('map')...`, clearly commented, with the free-tier link and the public-vs-secret token distinction spelled out inline so it's self-explanatory without needing to come back and ask again.

**What changed in the code.** `BASE_LAYERS` (previously always Esri satellite-hybrid / OpenStreetMap street / Esri topo) is now built by a ternary on a new `MAPBOX_ENABLED` flag (`true` only when `MAPBOX_TOKEN` starts with `"pk."` -- the placeholder text and an accidentally-pasted secret token both evaluate to `false`, so the app can't be left broken or misconfigured by an incomplete or wrong paste). When enabled, all three basemaps switch to Mapbox's own styles via their Static Tiles API -- `satellite-streets-v12`, `streets-v12`, `outdoors-v12`, the closest match to the existing satellite/street/topo trio -- built by a small `mapboxLayer(styleId)` helper. Mapbox's style tiles are served at 512×512 (`@2x`) rather than Leaflet's default 256×256 assumption, so `tileSize:512` and `zoomOffset:-1` are set on every Mapbox layer -- the standard, documented adjustment for Leaflet to line these up at the correct zoom level; skipping it is the most common way this exact integration silently renders at the wrong scale. When `MAPBOX_ENABLED` is `false` (the out-of-the-box state, and permanently so if Karim never fills the token in), `BASE_LAYERS` falls back byte-for-byte to the same Esri/OpenStreetMap layers this app has used since v11/v36 -- nothing else in the app (the basemap toggle buttons, `restyleCrownParcelLayer()`'s satellite-contrast check, etc.) needed to change, since they all key off `BASE_LAYERS`'s `satellite`/`street`/`topo` properties generically, not which underlying tile source is behind them.

**Verification.** The `MAPBOX_ENABLED`/`mapboxLayer()` logic was extracted and unit-tested standalone in a Node `vm` sandbox against a stub `L.tileLayer` -- 5 assertions: the placeholder token correctly leaves Mapbox disabled; a real `pk.`-prefixed token enables it; `mapboxLayer()` builds the exact expected tile URL (style id + token correctly interpolated); the URL carries `tileSize:512`/`zoomOffset:-1`; and -- specifically checking the failure mode of pasting the wrong *kind* of token -- an `sk.`-prefixed secret token does **not** enable Mapbox, staying safely on the working Esri/OpenStreetMap fallback rather than trying (and failing) to use a token type Mapbox's tile API doesn't accept for this purpose anyway. `node --check` (via full-file `new Function()` extraction) confirmed no syntax errors.

**Not yet visually confirmed** — since this needs Karim's own token to test against a live Mapbox tile server, the enabled path (`MAPBOX_ENABLED === true`) has only been verified at the logic/URL-construction level above, not by actually loading a Mapbox tile in a browser. Worth a look once a real token is pasted in: confirm all three basemap buttons render correctly and at the right zoom/scale, and that the attribution line (now crediting Mapbox + OpenStreetMap instead of Esri) shows up correctly in Leaflet's attribution control.

## v41: full-screen map with collapsible bottom report drawer and a translucent side checklist drawer
Karim's request: *"let's make the entire screen the map with a collapsable information toolbar on the bottom and a collapsable checklist on the side but make the checklist slightly transparen[t]."* Mid-turn he also asked about switching to Mapbox maps — that's on hold pending a Mapbox access token from his own account (a free Mapbox account/token is required to use their tiles at all; explained the free tier covers up to 50,000 map loads/month, comfortably enough for personal use, and asked him to paste a token here whenever he has one — creating the account itself isn't something this tool does on his behalf). This section covers only the layout redesign.

**What changed.** The header, legal-disclaimer bar, coordinate-entry controls, the side report/checklist column, and the footer — previously five stacked page sections squeezing the map into the remaining space — are now three floating panels over an edge-to-edge map:
- A slim **top bar** (title, "About this tool" info button, lat/lng inputs, the Check button, the current coordinate readout, and the "Not legal advice" disclaimer button) — always visible, not collapsible, since entering a location is the core interaction; semi-transparent navy with a blur so the map still reads through it.
- A **bottom drawer** for the location report (verdict + every data card, unchanged), collapsed by default down to a one-line handle. It opens itself automatically the moment a location check starts (and again once the report is ready, in case it had been manually collapsed mid-query) — the same report Karim used to see permanently in the side column now surfaces itself right when there's something to show, rather than needing to be found. The collapsed handle shows a live one-line summary (colour-coded dot + verdict label, e.g. "🛑 Significant red flags") so the gist is visible even without expanding it. The old footer's "Data sources & attribution" info button moved to the bottom of this drawer's expanded body.
- A **side drawer** for the full checklist (AUTO + MANUAL items, unchanged), collapsed by default down to a slim vertical tab on the right edge ("✅ Checklist"), sliding open to 360px wide (88vw on a phone) with its own ✕ close button. Per Karim's "slightly transparent" instruction, both the drawer's own background and the checklist cards inside it use a translucent white (~86% and ~60% opacity respectively) over a backdrop blur, rather than the fully opaque white every other panel in the app uses — the map is meant to be visible through it while it's open, not just visible around its edges.

**Under the hood.** `#mapWrap`/`#map` switched from a flex-layout column to `position:fixed;inset:0`, filling the viewport; `body` gained `overflow:hidden` since the page itself no longer scrolls (each drawer scrolls its own content instead). `#mapWrap` was given an explicit `z-index` specifically so it forms its own stacking context -- without that, Leaflet's own internal `z-index:1000` on its control corners (zoom control, the v40 draw toolbar) would otherwise render on top of the new top bar/drawers despite their higher-looking z-index values, since an unstacked `position:fixed` element doesn't contain its descendants' z-index on its own. Every existing floating map control (basemap toggle, Reveal Crown Land/Potential Spots buttons, the map legend, the v40 markup panel, Leaflet's own zoom control and draw toolbar) needed its position nudged clear of the new top bar/bottom drawer's collapsed handle -- confirmed by inspection of each one's fixed pixel offset, not guessed.

**Verification.** `node --check` (via full-file `new Function()` extraction) confirmed no syntax errors after every edit in this round. Every `id` referenced anywhere in the JS (43 unique ids) was confirmed to resolve to exactly one element in the HTML via a small script -- catches exactly the kind of "renamed an element but forgot a `getElementById`" bug a big structural reshuffle like this one risks. A `<div>`/`</div>` balance check across the whole file (186 open, 186 close, running depth never negative) confirmed the restructuring didn't leave a stray or missing closing tag, which it did catch and which was fixed mid-round (one leftover `</div>` from the old `.layout` wrapper).

**Not yet visually confirmed in a live browser** — same standing caveat as most layout work this session, and especially relevant here since this is the app's biggest structural change yet: the actual pixel alignment (does the map's zoom control/draw toolbar really clear the top bar with no gap or overlap at every screen width, does the bottom drawer's `max-height:64vh` feel right with a long report open, does the side drawer's translucency read as "slightly transparent" and not "hard to read" against a busy satellite view underneath) can only really be judged by looking at it. Worth a pass across both a wide desktop window and a narrow phone-width one, checking: the top bar wraps sensibly instead of overlapping itself; the bottom drawer auto-opens on a click and its collapsed one-line summary updates correctly; the side checklist tab opens/closes and its translucency looks intentional rather than illegible over a light section of satellite imagery; and that dragging/drawing markup (v40) still works with the map controls in their new positions.

## v40: UI cleanup pass, plus onX-style personal map markup (pins/lines/areas)
Karim's request: *"now let's clean up the app, take out unecessary text and condense things into collapsables as well as make the app more easy on the eyes. The idea is at a glance i's easy to read but if the user is to want more information they can go as deep as they want. Also let's add in a feature where you can add/edit things on the map similar to how onx has it setup."* Clarified scope up front on the markup feature: Karim chose the full onX-style toolkit (pins, lines, **and** areas — not pins-only) with both browser auto-save **and** a manual export/import file (not auto-save alone), so markup can be backed up or carried to another browser/device.

**Cleanup part.** Most of the app's "glance first, dig deeper on click" structure was already in place from earlier rounds — report cards already default-collapsed to a title + one-line status pill with the field-by-field breakdown behind a "Details" toggle (v22), and the map legend already starts collapsed (v22). The one place that had never gotten this treatment was the **manual checklist**: all 10 items printed their full explanatory paragraph inline, permanently, making the checklist the single largest block of always-visible text on the page. Each `MANUAL_ITEMS` entry was restructured from one `text` blob into `title` (bold heading), `summary` (a short, always-visible one-liner), and `full` (the complete original explanation, including links, now behind a small inline "More ▾" toggle — same expand/collapse pattern as the report cards' "Details", scoped per-item instead of per-card). A trivial item with nothing more to say than its summary (the PAL/safe-storage item) has no `full` at all and simply skips the toggle, mirroring how a report card with nothing beyond its pill already skips the "Details" button. The one item with dynamic, location-specific content (the bylaw item, which swaps in a live-fetched-bylaw notice or a jurisdiction-specific search link once a spot is clicked) keeps that override entirely in the always-visible summary line, since that's exactly the kind of thing worth seeing at a glance; the static "why this matters" background paragraph moved to "More" like every other item. A shared `manualChecklistItemHtml(item, summaryOverride)` helper now backs both the initial static render and the post-lookup AUTO+MANUAL render, so the two can't drift out of sync the way two independent template strings previously could.

**Markup part — onX-style pins, lines, and areas.** Added the [Leaflet.draw](https://github.com/Leaflet/Leaflet.draw) plugin (cdnjs, confirmed real and at the exact version/path used, same as every other CDN dependency this app already loads) for the actual draw/reshape/delete map interactions, rather than hand-rolling vertex editing and drag-to-reshape from scratch -- a mature, widely-used Leaflet plugin, and outside this app's own scope to reimplement. A new draw toolbar (top-left of the map, restyled lightly to sit with the app's own look) offers three tools -- marker, line, area -- with circle/rectangle/circlemarker turned off since Karim's scope was specifically pins/lines/areas. Every new shape is added to its own `markupLayer` (a Leaflet `FeatureGroup`, completely separate from every BC data overlay), immediately opens a small editor modal to name it and add notes (same the moment-you-drop-it flow onX uses), and offers 8 preset colours. A "My Markup" panel (bottom-right of the map, collapsed by default) lists everything added, with click-to-jump-to and an edit (✎) button per row; each markup shape's own map popup also has an Edit button. Deleting is available either via Leaflet.draw's own trash tool (multi-select on the map) or the editor modal's Delete button. Every markup layer sets `bubblingMouseEvents:false`, the same fix v33 applied to every other clickable map layer this app draws, so clicking your own pin doesn't also re-run the location lookup underneath it.

**Persistence.** Every add/edit/move/delete auto-saves the full markup set (as a GeoJSON `FeatureCollection`, one Feature per pin/line/area with its name/notes/colour in `properties`) to `localStorage`, keyed separately from anything else this app stores, wrapped in try/catch so a private-browsing tab or a full storage quota degrades to "works this session, doesn't persist" instead of throwing. On load, the same GeoJSON is read back and rebuilt into real Leaflet layers (marker/polyline/polygon chosen by geometry type, coordinates flipped from GeoJSON's `[lng,lat]` to Leaflet's `[lat,lng]`). Per Karim's answer to the persistence question, an **Export** button downloads that same `FeatureCollection` as a small `.json` file, and an **Import** button reads one back in -- import always regenerates IDs on the way in rather than trusting the file's own, so importing a second time (or a file from another browser/device) always merges in additively and can never silently collide with or overwrite markup already saved locally.

**Verification.** *Cleanup*, via the real extracted `manualChecklistItemHtml()` in a Node `vm` sandbox — 5 assertions: title + default summary render correctly; the "More" toggle and full text appear when `item.full` is set; no toggle renders for the trivial no-`full` item; a `summaryOverride` (the bylaw item's dynamic case) replaces the default summary while the static full text still shows underneath — **all 5 passed**. *Markup logic*, via the real extracted `markupUid`, `markupDefaultName`, `markupKindIcon`, `addMarkupItem`, `markupItemToFeature`, `markupPopupHtml`, `layerFromMarkupFeature`, and `loadMarkupFromFeatureCollection` in a Node `vm` sandbox against hand-rolled Leaflet stubs (fake `L.marker`/`L.polyline`/`L.polygon`/`L.FeatureGroup`/`L.divIcon` recording what they were called with, since real Leaflet needs a real DOM/canvas this test harness doesn't have) — 30 assertions: unique-ID generation across 200 calls; per-kind default-name numbering (Pin 1, Pin 2, Line 1, Area 1 — independent counters); `addMarkupItem` sets `bubblingMouseEvents:false`, applies a coloured `divIcon` to a marker, adds the layer to the `FeatureGroup`, and keeps a supplied name/colour/empty-string name; the marker→GeoJSON round trip preserves `[lng,lat]` order and carries name/colour into `properties`; the popup HTML includes the item's name, correctly escapes and line-breaks notes, and wires its Edit button to the right item ID; `layerFromMarkupFeature` picks the right Leaflet constructor per GeoJSON geometry type (Point/LineString/Polygon) with coordinates correctly flipped back to `[lat,lng]`, and returns `null` for an unsupported type or missing geometry rather than throwing; loading a `FeatureCollection` keeps each feature's saved ID by default but always assigns a fresh one when `regenerateIds:true` (the import path) — **all 30 passed**. `node --check` (via full-file `new Function()` extraction) on the full inline script confirmed no syntax errors after every edit in this round. Every new interactive HTML element referenced by ID in the new JS (`markupPanel`, `markupList`, `markupModalOverlay`, all editor-modal inputs/buttons, export/import controls, etc.) was confirmed present exactly once in the page's HTML via `grep`.

**Not yet visually confirmed in a live browser** — same standing caveat as most features this session, and more so than usual here: the markup feature's DOM/canvas-dependent pieces (the draw toolbar's actual click-to-place/click-to-reshape interactions, the Leaflet.draw edit/delete toolbar, the divIcon pin rendering, the modal's own open/close/focus behaviour) can't be exercised by a Node `vm` sandbox the way this app's other map-drawing features have been, since they need a real browser DOM and canvas — only the pure data/logic layer underneath them was unit-tested. Worth a real end-to-end pass: drop a pin, name it, drag it via the edit tool, delete it via the trash tool, draw a line and an area, reload the page and confirm everything reappears from local storage, then Export and re-Import the file and confirm it comes back correctly (with fresh IDs, not colliding with what's already there).

## v39: motor vehicle closures from BC's own Wildlife Act data, as a public-data substitute for the "roadblocks" part of v38's declined request
Karim's follow-up after v38 declined to scrape backroadstatus.com: *"For now let's use the public data for this then."* This targets specifically the "roadblocks, and impasses" part of his original combined v38 request — the road-quality/degradation color-coding (green/yellow/red) and physical impasses (washouts) parts are **not** covered by this: no public BC dataset equivalent to those was found, researched below.

**Research, not a guess.** Went looking for an official BC source for road closures/restrictions before writing any code. Found and confirmed live (via `DescribeFeatureType` + a sample `GetFeature`, not assumed from documentation) two DataBC WFS layers under the Wildlife Act's Motor Vehicle Prohibition Regulation (WAA_MVPR): `WHSE_WILDLIFE_MANAGEMENT.WAA_MVPR_ROUTES_SP` (linear — named roads/routes with an access-status/date range, e.g. seasonally or permanently closed to motor vehicles) and `WHSE_WILDLIFE_MANAGEMENT.WAA_MVPR_AREAS_SP` (polygon — whole areas closed to motor vehicle access, typically for wildlife/hunting-season reasons). This is a genuinely different thing from backroadstatus.com's community road-condition reports: it's a **legal closure/restriction feed** under provincial regulation (why a route or area is closed, and whether accessing it by vehicle would be against the Wildlife Act), not a physical road-condition report (it won't show a washout, a locked private gate, or a deactivated road that's otherwise still legally open to drive). That distinction is stated explicitly in the app's own UI (popup, report card, and legend) so it isn't mistaken for backroadstatus.com-style coverage.

**What changed in the code.** Two new `LAYERS` entries: `mvprRoutes` (`DWITHIN`, same 1 km `SEARCH_RADIUS_M` as other proximity layers — appropriate for a linear route) and `mvprAreas` (`INTERSECTS` — a polygon either contains the clicked point or it doesn't, same pattern already used for parks/municipalities). Both queried alongside everything else in `runLookup()`'s existing `Promise.all` batch. A new `buildMvprList(mvprRoutesR, mvprAreasR)` merges the two layers into one flat list, each entry tagged `kind: "route"` or `"area"` with its access status, road/area name, effective/expiry dates, region, and a `closed` boolean (routes: derived from `ACCESS_STATUS` text containing "closed"; areas: always `true`, since every returned area record is by definition a closed area). `renderMapOverlays()` draws routes as lines (red/solid if closed, olive/dashed if open) and areas as a filled polygon (red, dashed border) — each with a popup (`mvprPopup()`) showing the same fields plus the "not a road-condition report" caveat. A new report card ("Motor vehicle closures (Wildlife Act)") follows the existing `cardHTML()` pattern, lists up to 6 records with a "+N more" overflow line, and feeds the verdict: being inside a closed **area** is a hard red flag (motor vehicle access there is a Wildlife Act violation, not just inadvisable), nearby closed **routes** are an amber advisory (check before planning vehicle access), and nearby open routes with no closures at all get a green mention. The map legend gained matching swatches and an explanatory note repeating the "legal closure feed, not physical road-condition data" caveat.

**Verification.** *Unit tests*, via the real extracted `buildMvprList()`/`mvprPopup()` source in a Node `vm` sandbox, run against realistic field data (an open named route, a closed route with a PDF reference and date range, and a closed hunting-season area with an exemption note), plus an error-path check — 25 assertions: correct entry count and merge across both layers; open vs. closed routes correctly flagged via `ACCESS_STATUS` text; areas always flagged closed; road name, access status, date range, region, and PDF reference all captured correctly; `ok:false` inputs (both layers, and one layer) handled gracefully with no throw; and the rendered popup HTML for all three record types contains the right name, status, color-coded status styling, dates, exemption text, prohibition type, and the "not a physical road-condition report" caveat — **all 25 passed**. `node --check` (via full-file `new Function()` extraction) on the full inline script confirmed no syntax errors.

**Not yet visually confirmed against real BC data** — same standing caveat as most features this session: logic/rendering-tested via Node `vm` against realistic but hand-built field data, not yet eyeballed against a real MVPR closure record in a live browser. Worth a specific real-world check: click near a known seasonal wildlife closure (these are common near winter range areas) and confirm the report card, map line/polygon, and red/amber verdict reasoning all read correctly together.

**Still open — no public-data source found:** road-quality/degradation color-coding (green/mild/severe, the other half of Karim's original v38 request) and physical impasses (washouts, downed trees, etc.) have no BC government open-data equivalent found through this research pass. Those remain either backroadstatus.com-only (declined per v38's ToS finding) or a manual, Karim-entered option if he wants to revisit it later.

## v38: solid road lines shipped; pulling road conditions/gates/impasses from backroadstatus.com declined (ToS)
Karim's request had two parts: *"lets make the road line solid instead of dashed"* and *"let's grab the data from backroad status to label them in terms of quality. Green for good, yellow for mild degridation and red for severe degradation. Let's also grab roadblocks, and impasses."*

**Solid road lines -- done.** Removed `dashArray:"5 4"` from the forest service road layer's style in `renderMapOverlays()` (it now just has `color`/`weight`), and changed the two forest-service-road legend swatches (active/retired) from `border-style:dashed` to `solid` to match. Small, low-risk, purely cosmetic change; `node --check` on the full extracted inline script confirmed no syntax errors.

**Pulling backroadstatus.com's road-condition ratings, gates, and impassable sections -- declined, not built.** Investigated feasibility first (via the connected Chrome browser, logged into backroadstatus.com from the v36 work): their interactive map (`/explore`) requires login; their homepage's "Recent Bulletins"/"Road Updates" text (the same shape of data -- per-road, per-km-range condition text) renders even logged out, but appears to be server-rendered directly into the page HTML, not served from any discoverable public JSON API (searched their `bundle.js` for API routes -- found only unrelated ones like `/api/paths/search`, `/api/zones/within`, nothing bulletin/condition/gate-shaped). More importantly, their own Terms of Service (read in full) explicitly states access is for personal use only and that a user "must not... Reproduce, duplicate or copy material from Service Road Atlas" or "Redistribute content" from the site. Building an automated integration that regularly pulls their road-condition ratings, gate locations, and impassable-section data into a separate app is exactly the kind of reproduction/duplication their terms rule out, regardless of it being for personal use -- so this wasn't built. Presented this finding to Karim rather than proceeding or guessing at a technical workaround.

**What's still open:** if Karim wants road-condition color-coding without relying on backroadstatus.com's own dataset, the options worth considering next time are (a) a manual per-visit annotation Karim enters himself after checking backroadstatus.com (or the road in person) -- consistent with personal browsing use of their site, not automated reproduction of it -- or (b) checking whether BC itself publishes any official road-hazard/closure dataset independent of Service Road Atlas's own community layer (not yet researched). Neither has been scoped or built; this is a decision point for Karim, not a pending implementation task.

## v37: only pull cutblock data with a start date after 1990
Karim's request: *"now let's only pull cut block data that has a start date of after 1990."* Clarified one scope question before implementing: whether planned-but-not-yet-started blocks (no start date at all, only a planned harvest date from FTEN) should also be excluded — Karim chose to keep them, since they're current/future activity, not old history.

**The fix.** Added a shared `CUTBLOCK_START_DATE_CQL` constant -- `(DISTURBANCE_START_DATE IS NULL OR DISTURBANCE_START_DATE > '1990-01-01')` -- applied at the query level (not filtered client-side after the fact) via a new `extraFilter` property on `LAYERS.cutblock` (RESULTS RSLT_OPENING_SVW) and `LAYERS.cutblockPlan` (FTEN FTEN_CUT_BLOCK_POLY_SVW), the two layers `buildCutblockList()` merges into the cutblock report. `cqlFor()` now ANDs a layer's `extraFilter` onto its spatial predicate when one is set, so this stays purely additive for every other layer (parcels, parks, tenures, recreation, etc.) -- none of them set `extraFilter`, so their queries are byte-for-byte unchanged. Applied identically to both layers so an old, long-since-regrown block can't slip back in as an FTEN-only entry just because RESULTS excluded it. The `harvest` layer (VEG_CONSOLIDATED_CUT_BLOCKS_SP, used only to backfill `HARVEST_END_DATE` onto entries that already passed the cutblock/cutblockPlan filter) was deliberately left unfiltered -- it can't introduce a new entry on its own, so filtering it wouldn't change what's shown, just what's fetched, and there was no need to take on the risk of guessing whether that layer even has the same field.

**Verification.** `DISTURBANCE_START_DATE`'s presence on both layers, and the CQL comparison syntax itself, were confirmed live against BC's real WFS server (through the newly-connected Chrome browser, not the sandbox's restricted fetch tool) before writing any code -- including a control test with a stricter 2020 threshold that visibly dropped the FTEN feature count from 20 to 8 at the same point, confirming the filter has real effect and isn't silently ignored. After implementing, the app's actual `cqlFor()`/`wfsUrl()` functions were extracted and unit-tested (4 assertions: the cutblock and cutblockPlan filters combine correctly with DWITHIN via AND, the untouched `harvest` and `parcel` layers are byte-for-byte unaffected) -- all passed. The exact URL those functions generate for a real query was then fetched live through the connected browser one more time as an end-to-end check -- HTTP 200, real results back. `node --check` on the full extracted inline script confirmed no syntax errors.

**Not yet visually confirmed against real BC data inside the running app** -- same standing caveat as most features this session, though the underlying query and filter syntax are unusually well-grounded here (confirmed live at every step, not assumed). What's unconfirmed is only whether the report/map, end to end in a browser, now correctly omits pre-1990 cutblocks while still showing planned-only ones with no start date -- worth a spot check at a location known to have some older logging history nearby.

## v36: added a Topo basemap, and an inconclusive real-data investigation into a Life Cycle "n/a" report
Two separate threads this round.

**Basemap request.** Karim's request: *"Can add a map type and use the same one as backroad status"* — followed by connecting both a desktop browser pane and, later, the Claude in Chrome extension so this could be checked directly against the live site (he logged into backroadstatus.com himself both times; no credentials were ever entered on his behalf). Once connected, the live site's own `bundle.js` was fetched and searched directly (not guessed) for tile/style URLs: backroadstatus.com's default map is MapLibre GL (WebGL vector tiles, confirmed via `maplibre-gl.css` and matching canvas elements) served from their own self-hosted tile infrastructure (`tiles.backroadstatus.com/canada-osm`, `canada-terrain`) — not something this Leaflet-based, raster-tile app can point at from outside their infrastructure; that would mean rebuilding this app's map on MapLibre GL and hosting equivalent vector tiles, a much larger change than "add a map type." Their own code was also found to reference Esri's World Topographic Map (`server.arcgisonline.com/.../World_Topo_Map`) as one of their own basemap options -- a free, no-signup raster source in the same family Leaflet already renders here for Satellite. Presented this tradeoff to Karim directly (exact match isn't feasible without a much bigger rebuild; Esri Topo is a legitimate close match, not a guess) and he chose to proceed with it.

**The fix.** Added a third `BASE_LAYERS` entry, `topo` (Esri World Topographic Map tiles), and a matching "⛰️ Topo" button in the basemap toggle, alongside the existing Satellite/Street. The toggle's click-handling code was already generic (keyed off each button's `data-base` attribute, looping over whichever layers exist in `BASE_LAYERS`), so no logic changes were needed there -- purely additive. `crownParcelStyle()`'s only base-dependent behavior (`isSat = currentBase === 'satellite'`) already falls through correctly for any non-satellite base, Topo included, so Crown/untitled parcel styling on Reveal Crown Land needed no changes either.

**Verification.** `node --check` on the full extracted inline script confirmed no syntax errors. The tile source itself was confirmed live (not assumed) via a real `fetch()` from the connected Chrome browser against the exact tile URL template used in code -- HTTP 200, real JPEG tile data returned. Not yet visually confirmed inside this app itself (i.e. clicking the new Topo button and checking it renders/toggles correctly end-to-end in the live map) -- worth a quick look next time the app's open in a browser.

**Investigation into the Life Cycle "n/a" report (no code change; set aside, not resolved).** Karim flagged a specific real-world case: the map/report showed "life cycle n/a" for Opening 1721162, while geo.ca showed it as RETIRED. This was investigated directly against BC's live WFS data (both from this sandbox, hitting the parens-in-URL limitation documented earlier, and later through the connected Chrome browser, which has no such restriction since it's a real browser making real requests): confirmed BC's live FTEN_CUT_BLOCK_POLY_SVW data does have LIFE_CYCLE_STATUS_CODE=RETIRED for this opening (CUT_BLOCK_ID "9-2", Lower Squamish); confirmed both RESULTS' and FTEN's polygons for this opening are geometrically co-located (centroids ~30m apart); confirmed, by replaying the app's actual `DWITHIN(...,1000,meters)` query against both layers from a point on top of this block through the real browser, that BOTH layers correctly return this exact feature; and confirmed, by extracting and unit-testing the app's real `buildCutblockList()` merge function against this exact real data, that it correctly produces `lifeCycleStatus: "RETIRED"` -- i.e. every piece individually checks out. Karim then tried to reproduce the original "n/a" report and could not. Given the merge logic is confirmed correct against real data and the issue wasn't reproducible, this is being set aside rather than chased further blind -- most likely explanation is a boundary/precision edge case at the original click's exact distance from the block (RESULTS' and FTEN's independently-traced polygon boundaries differ by a few meters, which could flip a borderline "just inside vs. just outside 1 km" case for one layer but not the other), or it was a stale report from before some earlier fix this session. Worth revisiting only if it recurs with a reproducible set of coordinates.
